I mean, specifically the skin around my cheeks and my jawline. That’s the part I keep getting caught on, especially since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror. It’s like all of a sudden I can see this unevenness, or like it looks a little saggy to me in a way that I can’t stop noticing.\n\nI don’t really have the same kind of worry about other parts of my body, not like this. My husband says he doesn’t see what I’m talking about, or that it’s not noticeable, but when I’m standing there in that lighting it feels very obvious to me. So, yeah… it’s mainly my cheeks and jaw area that brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking your reflection in the mirror to look at the areas of your face that bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I’d like to admit. Most mornings, especially before I leave for the courthouse, I’ll stand in the bathroom and check my face from a few different angles, like straight on and then turning my head a little to see my cheeks and jawline. I’ll sort of lean in, then step back, because it looks different depending on the light.\n\nAnd then I usually end up putting on a little more makeup than I planned, just to try to even things out or make it less noticeable to me. It’s not like I spend hours and hours, but it has become part of the morning, and if I’m already running late it can make me feel kind of frustrated with myself.\n\nI’ve also noticed I compare my face to other women at work sometimes, which I don’t like admitting. Like in the courthouse or office, I’ll catch myself looking at someone around my age and wondering if their skin looks smoother or firmer than mine. And if someone wants to take a casual picture and I’m not feeling okay about how I look that day, I’ll try to avoid being in it or stand off to the side.\n\nDoctor: Are these concerns about your face affecting your daily activities or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not in a huge, life-falling-apart way, but it has started to get in the way a bit. I still get my work done and I don’t think anyone at the courthouse would say I’m not doing my job, but there have been mornings where I spend that extra time in the bathroom trying to get my makeup right or checking whether my cheeks look uneven, and then suddenly I’m rushing to get out the door. I’ve gotten to the courthouse later than I meant to a few times, kind of flustered, which is not how I like to start the day.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s affected little things too. I do yoga, and more than once I’ve missed the beginning of class because I couldn’t leave the house when I planned. It sounds silly when I say it, because it’s just a few minutes here and there, but it adds up and makes me feel annoyed with myself.\n\nWith people… I wouldn’t say it’s causing big fights or anything, but I’ve avoided being in some casual photos, like at book club or community events. If someone pulls out a phone and I’m already feeling bad about how my face looks that day, I’ll kind of step back or offer to take the picture instead. I don’t want to spend the whole time afterward worrying about how my face looked in it.\n\nDoctor: Do your concerns about your face and specifically the skin around your cheeks and jawline stay the same all the time, or do they vary from day to day?\n\nPatient: It varies some from day to day, but the concern itself is kind of… always there in the background now. Like, it really started getting stuck in my head after we put in that brighter bathroom mirror. Before that I might have noticed normal things here and there, but with that mirror I keep seeing the skin around my cheeks and jawline as uneven, or sort of saggy-looking.\n\nSome days it looks a little less obvious to me, especially if the lighting is softer or I’m not standing there examining it. But other days, especially in that bathroom light, it feels like it jumps out at me right away. My husband keeps saying he can’t really see what I’m talking about, which should make me feel better, I guess, but it doesn’t always. I’ll think, well, maybe he’s just being kind, or maybe he’s not looking closely enough.\n\nSo it’s not exactly the same intensity every single day, but it’s the same area I keep coming back to—my cheeks and jaw. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say these concerns about your face are causing you?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s… mild to maybe moderate on a bad morning? It’s not like I’m unable to function, and I do still get my work done. I’m not missing deadlines or anything like that. But it bothers me enough that it changes how my day starts.\n\nLike, I’ll mean to leave for the courthouse at a certain time, and then I’m still in the bathroom checking my face or trying to fix my makeup around my cheeks and jawline, and suddenly I’m rushing in feeling tense and annoyed with myself. That’s happened a few mornings.\n\nAnd with yoga, which is supposed to be relaxing, I’ve missed the start of class more than once because I couldn’t quite get myself out the door when I was caught up in how my face looked. Then I feel embarrassed walking in late, so it kind of ruins the point of going.\n\nSocially, it’s more subtle, but it’s there. I’ve skipped being in a few casual photos at book club or community events because I knew I’d probably look at the picture afterward and worry about my face. So… it’s not taking over everything, but it’s definitely causing distress and getting in the way more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided social interactions or events because of concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped going to things altogether or become isolated, but I have avoided parts of social situations because of it. Like at book club, if people want to take a casual picture, I’ve found myself stepping out of it or offering to take the photo instead, because I know I’ll end up looking at it later and worrying about my cheeks or my jawline.\n\nSame thing at community events—nothing dramatic, but I’ll kind of hang back if phones come out. It’s not that I don’t want to be with people, exactly. I do go, and I can enjoy myself, but if I’m already feeling self-conscious about my face that day, the idea of having a picture taken can really throw me off.\n\nAnd it’s affected things around getting out of the house too. I still get my work done, but I’ve spent extra time getting ready in the morning and then rushed into the courthouse a few times feeling flustered. I’ve also missed the start of yoga more than once because I couldn’t quite leave on time. So I’m not avoiding everything, but yes, it’s definitely made me pull back in small ways.\n\nDoctor: Is it always the same specific features of your face, like the unevenness or sagging of the skin around your cheeks and jawline, that bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s pretty much the same thing every time. It’s that area around my cheeks and jawline. I keep seeing the skin there as uneven, or like it’s starting to sag in a way that just looks really obvious to me.\n\nAnd I know I’ve mentioned the bathroom mirror, but that really is when it started to stick in my head more. Since we put in that brighter mirror, I feel like I notice every little shadow or texture there, and then I can’t quite unsee it. My husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, which is frustrating because part of me wants to believe him, but when I look, especially in that light, it feels very noticeable to me.\n\nSo yes, it’s not like I’m jumping from one feature to another all the time. It’s mostly the cheeks and jaw, that uneven or saggy-looking skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look normal to you if the skin around your cheeks and jawline were perfectly even and smooth?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yes. Or at least much closer to normal to me. It’s really that skin around my cheeks and jawline that I keep getting stuck on — like it looks uneven or a little saggy, especially in that brighter bathroom mirror we put in.\n\nBefore that mirror, I don’t think I focused on it nearly this much. Now I’ll look and it’s like every shadow or bit of texture stands out, and then I start thinking, “Is that what everyone else sees?” My husband says he honestly can’t really see what I’m talking about, and I know he’s trying to reassure me, but to me it still looks obvious.\n\nSo if that area were perfectly smooth and even, I do think I’d feel a lot less bothered by my face. Maybe I’d still find something small, I don’t know, but right now that’s the main thing I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your appearance if someone you trusted told you that the skin around your cheeks and jawline looked perfectly normal?\n\nPatient: I think I’d want to believe them, especially if it was someone I really trusted. Like when my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, part of me does try to take that in.\n\nBut honestly, yes, I think I would still be concerned, at least somewhat. Because when I look in that brighter bathroom mirror, I still see it — the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy. It’s hard because even if someone says it looks normal, my own eyes sort of argue with that, if that makes sense.\n\nI’m here because it’s gotten stuck in my head since we put in that mirror. Before that, I don’t think I was noticing it in the same way. Now I’ll catch the lighting a certain way and think, “No, that does look off.” So reassurance helps for a minute, but it doesn’t completely make the concern go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a possibility that your perception of your face might be different from how it actually looks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there’s a possibility. I mean, I’m trying to keep that in mind, because my husband keeps saying he really doesn’t see what I’m talking about, and I don’t think he’s just saying that to be nice.\n\nBut at the same time, when I’m standing in front of that brighter bathroom mirror, it feels very real to me. Ever since we put it in, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jawline, like it looks uneven or kind of saggy, and then I start wondering if that’s what my face actually looks like to everyone else. So I can understand, in a rational way, that maybe the lighting or the mirror is making me focus on it more than other people would. But when I see it, it’s hard to convince myself that I’m not seeing something real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would spend less time checking your face in the mirror if you couldn't see the specific area around your cheeks and jawline?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I probably would. I mean, that’s the part I keep zeroing in on, especially in the morning. I’ll go into the bathroom to get ready, and if I can see my cheeks and jawline clearly in that bright mirror, I end up checking from the front, then turning a little to one side, then the other side, and I’ll kind of lean in and look at the texture. It’s not like I plan to do that, it just happens.\n\nIf I couldn’t really see that area, I think there would be less for me to fixate on, if that makes sense. Most mornings I do end up spending extra time looking at my face and then putting on a little more makeup than I probably need before I leave for the courthouse. I tell myself I’m just making sure I look presentable for work, but really I’m trying to make that area look smoother or less noticeable.\n\nAnd it doesn’t always stop at the mirror. Sometimes at work I catch myself comparing my face to other women’s faces, like noticing whether their skin looks firmer or more even than mine. And if I’m already feeling bad about it, I don’t really want to be in casual pictures, because I worry that angle or lighting will show it. So yes, if that specific area wasn’t visible to me, I do think I’d check less. Maybe not none at all, but definitely less.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would decrease if you were to receive reassurance from a dermatologist or a plastic surgeon that your skin is within normal limits?\n\nPatient: I think it might help some, yes. I mean, if a dermatologist or plastic surgeon looked at me and said, very clearly, “Your skin is within normal limits,” I’d probably feel relieved in that moment. Especially because they would know what they’re looking at more than, you know, just my husband saying he doesn’t really see it.\n\nBut I don’t know if it would completely go away. That’s the part that bothers me. Because I’m here because ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jawline, and to me it looks uneven or kind of saggy. And even though my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, when I look in that mirror, I still see it. So I think reassurance from a professional would probably calm me down, maybe make me question myself more in a good way, but I’m not sure it would stop me from noticing it when I’m looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to push the thoughts about your face out of your mind once they start?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Once I notice it, it’s really hard to just let it go. It’s like my mind keeps circling back to that same area of my face — my cheeks and my jawline — and I keep thinking, does it look uneven, does it look saggy, is that how it looks to other people?\n\nAnd I know I’ve said this already, but it really seemed to start after we put in that brighter bathroom mirror. Before that, I don’t think I was thinking about it in quite the same way. But now, if I catch it in that lighting, it sort of sticks with me. Even if I walk away from the mirror, I’ll still be thinking about what I saw. My husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, and I try to remind myself of that, but it doesn’t always quiet the thought down. It’s like part of me believes him, and another part of me is still stuck on, “But I can see it.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control the thoughts about your face, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel that way sometimes. I’ll try to tell myself, “Just stop thinking about it, you need to get ready, you need to focus on work,” but then my mind goes right back to my face, especially that skin around my cheeks and jaw. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, it’s like I notice it so much more, like it looks uneven or a little saggy to me.\n\nAnd I know my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so I try to hold onto that. I’ll think, “Okay, maybe I’m making too much of it.” But then I see it again, or I remember how it looked in that mirror, and the thought just keeps coming back. It’s not constant every single second, but when it gets started, it’s harder to control than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thoughts about your face are popping into your head even when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do. Not all day nonstop, but they kind of pop up at times when I’m not deliberately trying to think about it. Like I’ll be doing something normal, or getting ready, and suddenly I’m back to thinking about that area around my cheeks and my jawline.\n\nI think a lot of it started with that brighter bathroom mirror we put in. Ever since then, I keep noticing the skin there, and to me it looks uneven or sort of saggy. And then even when I’m away from the mirror, the thought can still come into my head, like, “Is that really how my face looks?” My husband tells me he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, and I do try to remember that, but it still comes up on its own sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would completely disappear if you were able to achieve the appearance you consider ideal?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it would completely disappear. I mean, part of me wants to say yes, because if the skin around my cheeks and jaw looked the way I think it should look — smoother, more even, not like it’s sagging in that bathroom light — then maybe I wouldn’t keep getting caught on it.\n\nBut I’m also not totally sure, because this really started after we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, or at least that’s when I began noticing it so much. Now when I see my face there, I feel like I can’t unsee it, even though my husband keeps telling me he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about. So maybe if it looked “ideal” to me, I’d feel relieved… but I don’t know if my mind would just move on or if I’d still be checking whether it was uneven. It’s hard to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would decrease if you were shown a photo of yourself where the area around your cheeks and jawline looked perfectly smooth and even?\n\nPatient: Maybe for that moment, yes. If you showed me a photo where the skin around my cheeks and jaw looked really smooth and even, I think I’d probably feel relieved, like, “Okay, that looks normal, that looks fine.” Because that’s really what keeps pulling my attention lately — that area looking uneven or kind of saggy to me.\n\nBut I guess part of me would wonder if it was just the photo, or the lighting, because this all got so much worse after we put in that brighter bathroom mirror. In that mirror, I keep seeing it, and it’s hard not to focus on it. My husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, and I try to believe him, but when I look at myself, especially there, I still notice it. So a good photo might calm me down some, but I don’t know if it would make the concern go away completely.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

And if I’m already feeling bad about it, I don’t really want to be in casual pictures, because I worry that angle or lighting will show it. So yes, if that specific area wasn’t visible to me, I do think I’d check less. Maybe not none at all, but definitely less.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would decrease if you were to receive reassurance from a dermatologist or a plastic surgeon that your skin is within normal limits?\n\nPatient: I think it might help some, yes. I mean, if a dermatologist or plastic surgeon looked at me and said, very clearly, “Your skin is within normal limits,” I’d probably feel relieved in that moment. Especially because they would know what they’re looking at more than, you know, just my husband saying he doesn’t really see it.\n\nBut I don’t know if it would completely go away. That’s the part that bothers me. Because I’m here because ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jawline, and to me it looks uneven or kind of saggy. And even though my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, when I look in that mirror, I still see it. So I think reassurance from a professional would probably calm me down, maybe make me question myself more in a good way, but I’m not sure it would stop me from noticing it when I’m looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to push the thoughts about your face out of your mind once they start?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Once I notice it, it’s really hard to just let it go. It’s like my mind keeps circling back to that same area of my face — my cheeks and my jawline — and I keep thinking, does it look uneven, does it look saggy, is that how it looks to other people?\n\nAnd I know I’ve said this already, but it really seemed to start after we put in that brighter bathroom mirror. Before that, I don’t think I was thinking about it in quite the same way. But now, if I catch it in that lighting, it sort of sticks with me. Even if I walk away from the mirror, I’ll still be thinking about what I saw. My husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, and I try to remind myself of that, but it doesn’t always quiet the thought down. It’s like part of me believes him, and another part of me is still stuck on, “But I can see it.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control the thoughts about your face, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel that way sometimes. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, it’s like I notice it so much more, like it looks uneven or a little saggy to me.\n\nAnd I know my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so I try to hold onto that. Yes… I mean, I think it might be. That’s part of why I’m here, honestly. I’ve skipped being in a few casual photos at book club or community events because I knew I’d probably look at the picture afterward and worry about my face. So… it’s not taking over everything, but it’s definitely causing distress and getting in the way more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided social interactions or events because of concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped going to things altogether or become isolated, but I have avoided parts of social situations because of it. Like at book club, if people want to take a casual picture, I’ve found myself stepping out of it or offering to take the photo instead, because I know I’ll end up looking at it later and worrying about my cheeks or my jawline.\n\nSame thing at community events—nothing dramatic, but I’ll kind of hang back if phones come out. It’s not that I don’t want to be with people, exactly. I do go, and I can enjoy myself, but if I’m already feeling self-conscious about my face that day, the idea of having a picture taken can really throw me off.\n\nAnd it’s affected things around getting out of the house too. It’s that area around my cheeks and jawline. I keep seeing the skin there as uneven, or like it’s starting to sag in a way that just looks really obvious to me.\n\nAnd I know I’ve mentioned the bathroom mirror, but that really is when it started to stick in my head more. Since we put in that brighter mirror, I feel like I notice every little shadow or texture there, and then I can’t quite unsee it. My husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, which is frustrating because part of me wants to believe him, but when I look, especially in that light, it feels very noticeable to me.\n\nSo yes, it’s not like I’m jumping from one feature to another all the time. It’s mostly the cheeks and jaw, that uneven or saggy-looking skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look normal to you if the skin around your cheeks and jawline were perfectly even and smooth?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yes. Or at least much closer to normal to me. It’s really that skin around my cheeks and jawline that I keep getting stuck on — like it looks uneven or a little saggy, especially in that brighter bathroom mirror we put in.\n\nBefore that mirror, I don’t think I focused on it nearly this much. Now I’ll look and it’s like every shadow or bit of texture stands out, and then I start thinking, “Is that what everyone else sees?” My husband says he honestly can’t really see what I’m talking about, and I know he’s trying to reassure me, but to me it still looks obvious.\n\nSo if that area were perfectly smooth and even, I do think I’d feel a lot less bothered by my face. Maybe I’d still find something small, I don’t know, but right now that’s the main thing I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your appearance if someone you trusted told you that the skin around your cheeks and jawline looked perfectly normal?\n\nPatient: I think I’d want to believe them, especially if it was someone I really trusted. Like when my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, part of me does try to take that in.\n\nBut honestly, yes, I think I would still be concerned, at least somewhat. Because when I look in that brighter bathroom mirror, I still see it — the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy. It’s hard because even if someone says it looks normal, my own eyes sort of argue with that, if that makes sense.\n\nI’m here because it’s gotten stuck in my head since we put in that mirror. Before that, I don’t think I was noticing it in the same way. Now I’ll catch the lighting a certain way and think, “No, that does look off.” So reassurance helps for a minute, but it doesn’t completely make the concern go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a possibility that your perception of your face might be different from how it actually looks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there’s a possibility. I mean, I’m trying to keep that in mind, because my husband keeps saying he really doesn’t see what I’m talking about, and I don’t think he’s just saying that to be nice.\n\nBut at the same time, when I’m standing in front of that brighter bathroom mirror, it feels very real to me. Ever since we put it in, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jawline, like it looks uneven or kind of saggy, and then I start wondering if that’s what my face actually looks like to everyone else. So I can understand, in a rational way, that maybe the lighting or the mirror is making me focus on it more than other people would. But when I see it, it’s hard to convince myself that I’m not seeing something real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would spend less time checking your face in the mirror if you couldn't see the specific area around your cheeks and jawline?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I probably would. I mean, that’s the part I keep zeroing in on, especially in the morning. I’ll go into the bathroom to get ready, and if I can see my cheeks and jawline clearly in that bright mirror, I end up checking from the front, then turning a little to one side, then the other side, and I’ll kind of lean in and look at the texture. It’s not like I plan to do that, it just happens.\n\nIf I couldn’t really see that area, I think there would be less for me to fixate on, if that makes sense. Most mornings I do end up spending extra time looking at my face and then putting on a little more makeup than I probably need before I leave for the courthouse. I tell myself I’m just making sure I look presentable for work, but really I’m trying to make that area look smoother or less noticeable.\n\nAnd it doesn’t always stop at the mirror. Sometimes at work I catch myself comparing my face to other women’s faces, like noticing whether their skin looks firmer or more even than mine. And if I’m already feeling bad about it, I don’t really want to be in casual pictures, because I worry that angle or lighting will show it. So yes, if that specific area wasn’t visible to me, I do think I’d check less. Maybe not none at all, but definitely less.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would decrease if you were to receive reassurance from a dermatologist or a plastic surgeon that your skin is within normal limits?\n\nPatient: I think it might help some, yes. I mean, if a dermatologist or plastic surgeon looked at me and said, very clearly, “Your skin is within normal limits,” I’d probably feel relieved in that moment. Especially because they would know what they’re looking at more than, you know, just my husband saying he doesn’t really see it.\n\nBut I don’t know if it would completely go away. That’s the part that bothers me. Because I’m here because ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jawline, and to me it looks uneven or kind of saggy. And even though my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, when I look in that mirror, I still see it. So I think reassurance from a professional would probably calm me down, maybe make me question myself more in a good way, but I’m not sure it would stop me from noticing it when I’m looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to push the thoughts about your face out of your mind once they start?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Once I notice it, it’s really hard to just let it go. It’s like my mind keeps circling back to that same area of my face — my cheeks and my jawline — and I keep thinking, does it look uneven, does it look saggy, is that how it looks to other people?\n\nAnd I know I’ve said this already, but it really seemed to start after we put in that brighter bathroom mirror. Before that, I don’t think I was thinking about it in quite the same way. But now, if I catch it in that lighting, it sort of sticks with me. Even if I walk away from the mirror, I’ll still be thinking about what I saw. My husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, and I try to remind myself of that, but it doesn’t always quiet the thought down. It’s like part of me believes him, and another part of me is still stuck on, “But I can see it.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control the thoughts about your face, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel that way sometimes. I’ll try to tell myself, “Just stop thinking about it, you need to get ready, you need to focus on work,” but then my mind goes right back to my face, especially that skin around my cheeks and jaw. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, it’s like I notice it so much more, like it looks uneven or a little saggy to me.\n\nAnd I know my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so I try to hold onto that. I’ll think, “Okay, maybe I’m making too much of it.” But then I see it again, or I remember how it looked in that mirror, and the thought just keeps coming back. It’s not constant every single second, but when it gets started, it’s harder to control than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thoughts about your face are popping into your head even when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do. Not all day nonstop, but they kind of pop up at times when I’m not deliberately trying to think about it. Like I’ll be doing something normal, or getting ready, and suddenly I’m back to thinking about that area around my cheeks and my jawline.\n\nI think a lot of it started with that brighter bathroom mirror we put in. Ever since then, I keep noticing the skin there, and to me it looks uneven or sort of saggy. And then even when I’m away from the mirror, the thought can still come into my head, like, “Is that really how my face looks?” My husband tells me he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, and I do try to remember that, but it still comes up on its own sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would completely disappear if you were able to achieve the appearance you consider ideal?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it would completely disappear. I mean, part of me wants to say yes, because if the skin around my cheeks and jaw looked the way I think it should look — smoother, more even, not like it’s sagging in that bathroom light — then maybe I wouldn’t keep getting caught on it.\n\nBut I’m also not totally sure, because this really started after we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, or at least that’s when I began noticing it so much. Now when I see my face there, I feel like I can’t unsee it, even though my husband keeps telling me he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about. So maybe if it looked “ideal” to me, I’d feel relieved… but I don’t know if my mind would just move on or if I’d still be checking whether it was uneven. It’s hard to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face would decrease if you were shown a photo of yourself where the area around your cheeks and jawline looked perfectly smooth and even?\n\nPatient: Maybe for that moment, yes. If you showed me a photo where the skin around my cheeks and jaw looked really smooth and even, I think I’d probably feel relieved, like, “Okay, that looks normal, that looks fine.” Because that’s really what keeps pulling my attention lately — that area looking uneven or kind of saggy to me.\n\nBut I guess part of me would wonder if it was just the photo, or the lighting, because this all got so much worse after we put in that brighter bathroom mirror. In that mirror, I keep seeing it, and it’s hard not to focus on it. My husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, and I try to believe him, but when I look at myself, especially there, I still notice it. So a good photo might calm me down some, but I don’t know if it would make the concern go away completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face might be distorted in a way that doesn't match how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think it might be. That’s part of why I’m here, honestly. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep seeing this area around my cheeks and jaw, and to me the skin looks uneven, or kind of saggy, like it just doesn’t look right. And once I notice it, I have a hard time not seeing it.\n\nBut then my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, or at least not the way I’m seeing it. So that makes me wonder if maybe I’m seeing it more harshly than other people do. I don’t know if I’d say I fully believe that in the moment, because when I’m looking in that mirror it feels very obvious to me, but I can recognize that maybe my perception isn’t matching what other people are seeing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I think that’s possible, yes. I mean, I’m here because ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw in a way I didn’t before. To me it looks uneven, or kind of saggy, and then once I see it, it’s like my attention just goes right there every time.

And I know my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, or not in the way I’m describing it, so that does make me wonder if it’s more about how I’m feeling when I look at myself overall. Like maybe I’m already feeling not great about how I look, and then that area becomes the thing I latch onto. But in the moment, when I’m standing in front of that mirror, it really does look wrong to me, so it’s hard to separate what’s actually there from what I’m focusing on.

I’m not sure if it’s the lighting or if I’m just seeing something that’s always been there, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you've been worrying about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my face, really. I’ll think, well, maybe he’s just being kind, or maybe he’s not looking closely enough.\n\nSo it’s not exactly the same intensity every single day, but it’s the same area I keep coming back to—my cheeks and jaw. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say these concerns about your face are causing you?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s… mild to maybe moderate on a bad morning? It’s not like I’m unable to function, and I do still get my work done. I’m not missing deadlines or anything like that. But it bothers me enough that it changes how my day starts.\n\nLike, I’ll mean to leave for the courthouse at a certain time, and then I’m still in the bathroom checking my face or trying to fix my makeup around my cheeks and jawline, and suddenly I’m rushing in feeling tense and annoyed with myself. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped going to things altogether or become isolated, but I have avoided parts of social situations because of it. Like at book club, if people want to take a casual picture, I’ve found myself stepping out of it or offering to take the photo instead, because I know I’ll end up looking at it later and worrying about my cheeks or my jawline.\n\nSame thing at community events—nothing dramatic, but I’ll kind of hang back if phones come out. It’s not that I don’t want to be with people, exactly. I do go, and I can enjoy myself, but if I’m already feeling self-conscious about my face that day, the idea of having a picture taken can really throw me off.\n\nAnd it’s affected things around getting out of the house too. I still get my work done, but I’ve spent extra time getting ready in the morning and then rushed into the courthouse a few times feeling flustered. I’ve also missed the start of yoga more than once because I couldn’t quite leave on time. So I’m not avoiding everything, but yes, it’s definitely made me pull back in small ways.\n\nDoctor: Is it always the same specific features of your face, like the unevenness or sagging of the skin around your cheeks and jawline, that bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s pretty much the same thing every time. It’s that area around my cheeks and jawline. I keep seeing the skin there as uneven, or like it’s starting to sag in a way that just looks really obvious to me.\n\nAnd I know I’ve mentioned the bathroom mirror, but that really is when it started to stick in my head more. Since we put in that brighter mirror, I feel like I notice every little shadow or texture there, and then I can’t quite unsee it. My husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, which is frustrating because part of me wants to believe him, but when I look, especially in that light, it feels very noticeable to me.\n\nSo yes, it’s not like I’m jumping from one feature to another all the time. It’s mostly the cheeks and jaw, that uneven or saggy-looking skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look normal to you if the skin around your cheeks and jawline were perfectly even and smooth?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yes. Or at least much closer to normal to me. It’s really that skin around my cheeks and jawline that I keep getting stuck on — like it looks uneven or a little saggy, especially in that brighter bathroom mirror we put in.\n\nBefore that mirror, I don’t think I focused on it nearly this much. Now I’ll look and it’s like every shadow or bit of texture stands out, and then I start thinking, “Is that what everyone else sees?” My husband says he honestly can’t really see what I’m talking about, and I know he’s trying to reassure me, but to me it still looks obvious.\n\nSo if that area were perfectly smooth and even, I do think I’d feel a lot less bothered by my face. Maybe I’d still find something small, I don’t know, but right now that’s the main thing I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your appearance if someone you trusted told you that the skin around your cheeks and jawline looked perfectly normal?\n\nPatient: I think I’d want to believe them, especially if it was someone I really trusted. So I can understand, in a rational way, that maybe the lighting or the mirror is making me focus on it more than other people would. I mean, that’s the part I keep zeroing in on, especially in the morning. Most mornings I do end up spending extra time looking at my face and then putting on a little more makeup than I probably need before I leave for the courthouse. So yes, if that specific area wasn’t visible to me, I do think I’d check less. I mean, if a dermatologist or plastic surgeon looked at me and said, very clearly, “Your skin is within normal limits,” I’d probably feel relieved in that moment. Especially because they would know what they’re looking at more than, you know, just my husband saying he doesn’t really see it.\n\nBut I don’t know if it would completely go away. That’s the part that bothers me. And even though my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, when I look in that mirror, I still see it. So I think reassurance from a professional would probably calm me down, maybe make me question myself more in a good way, but I’m not sure it would stop me from noticing it when I’m looking at my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to push the thoughts about your face out of your mind once they start?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Once I notice it, it’s really hard to just let it go. It’s like my mind keeps circling back to that same area of my face — my cheeks and my jawline — and I keep thinking, does it look uneven, does it look saggy, is that how it looks to other people?\n\nAnd I know I’ve said this already, but it really seemed to start after we put in that brighter bathroom mirror. Even if I walk away from the mirror, I’ll still be thinking about what I saw. Ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, it’s like I notice it so much more, like it looks uneven or a little saggy to me.\n\nAnd I know my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so I try to hold onto that. Not all day nonstop, but they kind of pop up at times when I’m not deliberately trying to think about it. Ever since then, I keep noticing the skin there, and to me it looks uneven or sort of saggy. I mean, part of me wants to say yes, because if the skin around my cheeks and jaw looked the way I think it should look — smoother, more even, not like it’s sagging in that bathroom light — then maybe I wouldn’t keep getting caught on it.\n\nBut I’m also not totally sure, because this really started after we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, or at least that’s when I began noticing it so much.
